People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, even on my better days lately, I just can't shake this feeling I've been having. It's the people at my church, and also the ladies in my walking club. I just know they're watching me. They keep putting these little messages about me in the Sunday bulletin, or they slip things into what they say when we're out walking. 

We usually walk around the reservoir, you know, the one over by the old highway? But last week it rained so much we had to walk at the indoor mall instead. I really don't care for the mall, the floors are too shiny and I always worry I'm going to slip in my sneakers. Plus, the smell from that soft pretzel stand is just overwhelming. My husband used to love those pretzels, though. He'd buy one every single time we went to Sears. It's such a shame Sears closed down, I used to get all my appliances there, and you just can't find that kind of customer service anymore. 

But... oh, anyway. The messages. They just keep slipping these little hints about me into their conversations at the club and at church, and I just don't know why they're doing it.

Well, yes, sometimes I do. It usually happens when the house is just... completely quiet. I'll be sitting there, maybe doing a crossword puzzle, and I'll hear my name. Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.

Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.

But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23.3, F20.0, F25.9, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, even on my better days lately, I just can't shake this feeling I've been having. It's the people at my church, and also the ladies in my walking club. I just know they're watching me. They keep putting these little messages about me in the Sunday bulletin, or they slip things into what they say when we're out walking. \n\nWe usually walk around the reservoir, you know, the one over by the old highway? But last week it rained so much we had to walk at the indoor mall instead. I really don't care for the mall, the floors are too shiny and I always worry I'm going to slip in my sneakers. Plus, the smell from that soft pretzel stand is just overwhelming. My husband used to love those pretzels, though. He'd buy one every single time we went to Sears. It's such a shame Sears closed down, I used to get all my appliances there, and you just can't find that kind of customer service anymore. \n\nBut... oh, anyway. The messages. They just keep slipping these little hints about me into their conversations at the club and at church, and I just don't know why they're doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, sometimes I do. It usually happens when the house is just... completely quiet. I'll be sitting there, maybe doing a crossword puzzle, and I'll hear my name. Or just a few words, clear as day, coming from the kitchen or out on the back porch. And it sounds exactly like my friend Shirley's voice. It's so clear that I'll actually answer back, like, \"I'm in the living room, Shirley!\" before I realize... oh, she's not here. Nobody's here. \n\nShirley and I used to spend a lot of time on that porch. We'd sit out there with our iced tea—sweet tea, of course, the way she makes it with a pinch of baking soda so it doesn't get bitter. She swears by that trick, learned it from her grandmother who lived down in Georgia. Her grandmother had this beautiful old farmhouse with a wrap-around porch, the kind you just don't see being built anymore. They used real wood back then, not this composite stuff that looks like plastic. My son-in-law put a composite deck on his house, and I just think it gets far too hot on the feet in the summer... \n\nBut yes, I hear her voice, or sometimes just my name, when it's quiet.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\n[Last Question]\nAbout how long have you been having these experiences?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on since not long after my close friend died, about a year and five months ago.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.

name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don't have a plan to hurt myself, but I catch myself thinking more and more that it might be all right if I went to sleep and didn't wake up.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I was at my lowest, barely eating and thinking I had failed everyone, I was also hearing my friend's voice and believing church people were sending messages about me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of this whole stretch, the sadness, guilt, and loss of interest have been there in the background, even during times when the voices or suspicious thoughts were quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a spell of several weeks when I was getting dressed, eating a bit better, and not feeling so weighed down, but I still heard my name in the house and thought people at church were hinting about me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I still live at home, but I have stopped keeping up with quilting gatherings, walking club, and much of my gardening, and my daughter has started checking on my meals and appointments more often.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since not long after my close friend died, about a year and five months ago.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices, messages, and trouble keeping my thoughts straight have been happening for about eleven months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The deep sadness, guilt, and loss of interest have been with me for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.

They used real wood back then, not this composite stuff that looks like plastic. My son-in-law put a composite deck on his house, and I just think it gets far too hot on the feet in the summer... \n\nBut yes, I hear her voice, or sometimes just my name, when it's quiet.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.

It's such a shame Sears closed down, I used to get all my appliances there, and you just can't find that kind of customer service anymore. \n\nBut... oh, anyway. The messages. They just keep slipping these little hints about me into their conversations at the club and at church, and I just don't know why they're doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, sometimes I do. It usually happens when the house is just... completely quiet. I'll be sitting there, maybe doing a crossword puzzle, and I'll hear my name. Or just a few words, clear as day, coming from the kitchen or out on the back porch. And it sounds exactly like my friend Shirley's voice. It's so clear that I'll actually answer back, like, \"I'm in the living room, Shirley!\" before I realize... oh, she's not here. Nobody's here. \n\nShirley and I used to spend a lot of time on that porch. We'd sit out there with our iced tea—sweet tea, of course, the way she makes it with a pinch of baking soda so it doesn't get bitter. She swears by that trick, learned it from her grandmother who lived down in Georgia. Her grandmother had this beautiful old farmhouse with a wrap-around porch, the kind you just don't see being built anymore. They used real wood back then, not this composite stuff that looks like plastic. My son-in-law put a composite deck on his house, and I just think it gets far too hot on the feet in the summer... \n\nBut yes, I hear her voice, or sometimes just my name, when it's quiet.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from these messages and hearing Shirley’s voice, have you found it harder to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose it has been harder. I still live at home, you know, in the same house we bought in '85. It has that lovely wrap-around porch. But I just haven't been keeping up with my usual things. I used to go to my quilting gatherings every Wednesday. We'd sit around the big frame at the community center, all of us working on a different section. Helen always brought her famous lemon bars. She uses extra zest in the icing, that's her secret. I tried making them once, but I left them in the oven too long because the timer on my stove is broken. It just buzzes whenever it feels like it, completely unreliable. I really need to call the repairman, Mr. Henderson, but his truck is always parked outside the diner so I think he's mostly retired now. Anyway, I stopped going to quilting, and I haven't been to the walking club either. We used to walk the track at the high school, three miles every morning. The track is made of that bouncy rubber material, so it's easy on the knees. I bought a special pair of New Balance shoes just for it, the white ones with the blue N on the side. They have excellent arch support. But they're just gathering dust in the closet now. I haven't even been doing much of my gardening, which is a shame because the weeds are just taking over the hostas. My daughter, Sarah, she noticed. She's started coming over a lot more to check on my meals and make sure I'm getting to my appointments. She works at the bank downtown, the one with the big clock out front that chimes every hour. It's a very stressful job, so I feel bad that she has to worry about me on top of it all. But she insists on checking the fridge to see if I've eaten.

That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? They used real wood back then, not this composite stuff that looks like plastic. My son-in-law put a composite deck on his house, and I just think it gets far too hot on the feet in the summer... \n\nBut yes, I hear her voice, or sometimes just my name, when it's quiet.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these messages and hearing Shirley’s voice, have you found it harder to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been harder. I still live at home, you know, in the same house we bought in '85. It has that lovely wrap-around porch. But I just haven't been keeping up with my usual things. I used to go to my quilting gatherings every Wednesday. We'd sit around the big frame at the community center, all of us working on a different section. Helen always brought her famous lemon bars. She uses extra zest in the icing, that's her secret. I tried making them once, but I left them in the oven too long because the timer on my stove is broken. It just buzzes whenever it feels like it, completely unreliable. I really need to call the repairman, Mr. Henderson, but his truck is always parked outside the diner so I think he's mostly retired now. Anyway, I stopped going to quilting, and I haven't been to the walking club either. We used to walk the track at the high school, three miles every morning. The track is made of that bouncy rubber material, so it's easy on the knees. I bought a special pair of New Balance shoes just for it, the white ones with the blue N on the side. They have excellent arch support. But they're just gathering dust in the closet now. I haven't even been doing much of my gardening, which is a shame because the weeds are just taking over the hostas. My daughter, Sarah, she noticed. She's started coming over a lot more to check on my meals and make sure I'm getting to my appointments. She works at the bank downtown, the one with the big clock out front that chimes every hour. It's a very stressful job, so I feel bad that she has to worry about me on top of it all. But she insists on checking the fridge to see if I've eaten.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Controlling my thoughts? No, not controlling them... I don't feel like a puppet or anything. But I do feel... well, I came in today because even on my better days, when the sadness isn't so heavy, I just can't shake this awful feeling that people are watching me. Especially the people at church, and the ones from the walking club. I know I haven't been to the walking club in a while, but I still see them around town, you know? Like at the grocery store. I saw Brenda there last week in the produce section. She was thumping a cantaloupe, trying to see if it was ripe. You really have to smell the stem end to know for sure, thumping doesn't do much for melons. It works for watermelons, though. My father taught me that. He used to grow the biggest watermelons in our backyard, the Jubilee kind with the dark green stripes. We'd eat them out on the patio and spit the seeds into the grass. The grass was always so green back then... But anyway, Brenda gave me this look, and I just know they're talking about me. And the church bulletin! I've been finding these little messages in it. Just little phrases that seem directed right at me, or things people say during fellowship hour that have a hidden meaning. It's very unsettling. We always have coffee and those little powdered sugar donuts after the service. The donuts always leave white dust all over my good black wool coat. I have a lint roller, the kind with the sticky paper you peel off, but it never seems to get it all. I buy the replacement rolls in bulk at the warehouse club. Do you have a membership there? The savings on paper towels alone is worth the annual fee.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F25.9, F22, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, even on my better days lately, I just can't shake this feeling I've been having. It's the people at my church, and also the ladies in my walking club. I just know they're watching me. They keep putting these little messages about me in the Sunday bulletin, or they slip things into what they say when we're out walking. \n\nWe usually walk around the reservoir, you know, the one over by the old highway? But last week it rained so much we had to walk at the indoor mall instead. I really don't care for the mall, the floors are too shiny and I always worry I'm going to slip in my sneakers. Plus, the smell from that soft pretzel stand is just overwhelming. My husband used to love those pretzels, though. He'd buy one every single time we went to Sears. It's such a shame Sears closed down, I used to get all my appliances there, and you just can't find that kind of customer service anymore. \n\nBut... oh, anyway. The messages. They just keep slipping these little hints about me into their conversations at the club and at church, and I just don't know why they're doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, sometimes I do. It usually happens when the house is just... completely quiet. I'll be sitting there, maybe doing a crossword puzzle, and I'll hear my name. Or just a few words, clear as day, coming from the kitchen or out on the back porch. And it sounds exactly like my friend Shirley's voice. It's so clear that I'll actually answer back, like, \"I'm in the living room, Shirley!\" before I realize... oh, she's not here.
