I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these messages and hearing Shirley’s voice, have you found it harder to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been harder. I still live at home, you know, in the same house we bought in '85. It has that lovely wrap-around porch. But I just haven't been keeping up with my usual things. I used to go to my quilting gatherings every Wednesday. We'd sit around the big frame at the community center, all of us working on a different section. Helen always brought her famous lemon bars. She uses extra zest in the icing, that's her secret. I tried making them once, but I left them in the oven too long because the timer on my stove is broken. It just buzzes whenever it feels like it, completely unreliable. I really need to call the repairman, Mr. Henderson, but his truck is always parked outside the diner so I think he's mostly retired now. Anyway, I stopped going to quilting, and I haven't been to the walking club either. We used to walk the track at the high school, three miles every morning. The track is made of that bouncy rubber material, so it's easy on the knees. I bought a special pair of New Balance shoes just for it, the white ones with the blue N on the side. They have excellent arch support. But they're just gathering dust in the closet now. I haven't even been doing much of my gardening, which is a shame because the weeds are just taking over the hostas. My daughter, Sarah, she noticed. She's started coming over a lot more to check on my meals and make sure I'm getting to my appointments. She works at the bank downtown, the one with the big clock out front that chimes every hour. It's a very stressful job, so I feel bad that she has to worry about me on top of it all. But she insists on checking the fridge to see if I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? No, not controlling them... I don't feel like a puppet or anything. But I do feel... well, I came in today because even on my better days, when the sadness isn't so heavy, I just can't shake this awful feeling that people are watching me. Especially the people at church, and the ones from the walking club. I know I haven't been to the walking club in a while, but I still see them around town, you know? Like at the grocery store. I saw Brenda there last week in the produce section. She was thumping a cantaloupe, trying to see if it was ripe. You really have to smell the stem end to know for sure, thumping doesn't do much for melons. It works for watermelons, though. My father taught me that. He used to grow the biggest watermelons in our backyard, the Jubilee kind with the dark green stripes. We'd eat them out on the patio and spit the seeds into the grass. The grass was always so green back then... But anyway, Brenda gave me this look, and I just know they're talking about me. And the church bulletin! I've been finding these little messages in it. Just little phrases that seem directed right at me, or things people say during fellowship hour that have a hidden meaning. It's very unsettling. We always have coffee and those little powdered sugar donuts after the service. The donuts always leave white dust all over my good black wool coat. I have a lint roller, the kind with the sticky paper you peel off, but it never seems to get it all. I buy the replacement rolls in bulk at the warehouse club. Do you have a membership there? The savings on paper towels alone is worth the annual fee.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose I have. When I was at my absolute lowest, just feeling so heavy and sad every single day... barely eating a thing and thinking I had just failed everyone in my life... that was exactly when I was hearing Shirley's voice the most, and believing all those church people were sending messages about me. It all happened right together. 

The not eating was very unusual for me, because normally I love a good bowl of oatmeal in the morning. I buy the steel-cut oats, not those quick rolled ones. They take much longer to cook, you have to simmer them on the stove for a good twenty minutes, but the chewy texture is so much better. I like to put a little brown sugar and a splash of half-and-half in mine. The half-and-half comes in those little cardboard cartons, but they always seem to leak around the spout, don't they? It leaves a sticky ring on the refrigerator shelf. I actually mentioned it to the manager at the grocery store, a nice young man who always wears a red tie. His name tag says 'Kevin', but I'm pretty sure his real name is Kenneth because his mother came in once and called him Kenny. She was buying an awful lot of canned cat food. I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo, but she completely ruined my good living room drapes... 

But yes, the deep sadness and the voices and the messages, they were all happening at the exact same time.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F25.9, F22, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, even on my better days lately, I just can't shake this feeling I've been having. It's the people at my church, and also the ladies in my walking club. I just know they're watching me. They keep putting these little messages about me in the Sunday bulletin, or they slip things into what they say when we're out walking. \n\nWe usually walk around the reservoir, you know, the one over by the old highway? But last week it rained so much we had to walk at the indoor mall instead. I really don't care for the mall, the floors are too shiny and I always worry I'm going to slip in my sneakers. Plus, the smell from that soft pretzel stand is just overwhelming. My husband used to love those pretzels, though. He'd buy one every single time we went to Sears. It's such a shame Sears closed down, I used to get all my appliances there, and you just can't find that kind of customer service anymore. \n\nBut... oh, anyway. The messages. They just keep slipping these little hints about me into their conversations at the club and at church, and I just don't know why they're doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, sometimes I do. It usually happens when the house is just... completely quiet. I'll be sitting there, maybe doing a crossword puzzle, and I'll hear my name. Or just a few words, clear as day, coming from the kitchen or out on the back porch. And it sounds exactly like my friend Shirley's voice. It's so clear that I'll actually answer back, like, \"I'm in the living room, Shirley!\" before I realize... oh, she's not here. Nobody's here. \n\nShirley and I used to spend a lot of time on that porch. We'd sit out there with our iced tea—sweet tea, of course, the way she makes it with a pinch of baking soda so it doesn't get bitter. She swears by that trick, learned it from her grandmother who lived down in Georgia. Her grandmother had this beautiful old farmhouse with a wrap-around porch, the kind you just don't see being built anymore. They used real wood back then, not this composite stuff that looks like plastic. My son-in-law put a composite deck on his house, and I just think it gets far too hot on the feet in the summer... \n\nBut yes, I hear her voice, or sometimes just my name, when it's quiet.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these messages and hearing Shirley’s voice, have you found it harder to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been harder. I still live at home, you know, in the same house we bought in '85. It has that lovely wrap-around porch. But I just haven't been keeping up with my usual things. I used to go to my quilting gatherings every Wednesday. We'd sit around the big frame at the community center, all of us working on a different section. Helen always brought her famous lemon bars. She uses extra zest in the icing, that's her secret. I tried making them once, but I left them in the oven too long because the timer on my stove is broken. It just buzzes whenever it feels like it, completely unreliable. I really need to call the repairman, Mr. Henderson, but his truck is always parked outside the diner so I think he's mostly retired now. Anyway, I stopped going to quilting, and I haven't been to the walking club either. We used to walk the track at the high school, three miles every morning. The track is made of that bouncy rubber material, so it's easy on the knees. I bought a special pair of New Balance shoes just for it, the white ones with the blue N on the side. They have excellent arch support. But they're just gathering dust in the closet now. I haven't even been doing much of my gardening, which is a shame because the weeds are just taking over the hostas. My daughter, Sarah, she noticed. She's started coming over a lot more to check on my meals and make sure I'm getting to my appointments. She works at the bank downtown, the one with the big clock out front that chimes every hour. It's a very stressful job, so I feel bad that she has to worry about me on top of it all. But she insists on checking the fridge to see if I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? No, not controlling them... I don't feel like a puppet or anything. But I do feel... well, I came in today because even on my better days, when the sadness isn't so heavy, I just can't shake this awful feeling that people are watching me. Especially the people at church, and the ones from the walking club. I know I haven't been to the walking club in a while, but I still see them around town, you know? Like at the grocery store. I saw Brenda there last week in the produce section. She was thumping a cantaloupe, trying to see if it was ripe. You really have to smell the stem end to know for sure, thumping doesn't do much for melons. It works for watermelons, though. My father taught me that. He used to grow the biggest watermelons in our backyard, the Jubilee kind with the dark green stripes. We'd eat them out on the patio and spit the seeds into the grass. The grass was always so green back then... But anyway, Brenda gave me this look, and I just know they're talking about me. And the church bulletin! I've been finding these little messages in it. Just little phrases that seem directed right at me, or things people say during fellowship hour that have a hidden meaning. It's very unsettling. We always have coffee and those little powdered sugar donuts after the service. The donuts always leave white dust all over my good black wool coat. I have a lint roller, the kind with the sticky paper you peel off, but it never seems to get it all. I buy the replacement rolls in bulk at the warehouse club. Do you have a membership there? The savings on paper towels alone is worth the annual fee.\n\nDoctor: While these voices and messages have been happening, have you had a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. When I was at my absolute lowest, just feeling so heavy and sad every single day... barely eating a thing and thinking I had just failed everyone in my life... that was exactly when I was hearing Shirley's voice the most, and believing all those church people were sending messages about me. It all happened right together. \n\nThe not eating was very unusual for me, because normally I love a good bowl of oatmeal in the morning. I buy the steel-cut oats, not those quick rolled ones. They take much longer to cook, you have to simmer them on the stove for a good twenty minutes, but the chewy texture is so much better. I like to put a little brown sugar and a splash of half-and-half in mine. The half-and-half comes in those little cardboard cartons, but they always seem to leak around the spout, don't they? It leaves a sticky ring on the refrigerator shelf. I actually mentioned it to the manager at the grocery store, a nice young man who always wears a red tie. His name tag says 'Kevin', but I'm pretty sure his real name is Kenneth because his mother came in once and called him Kenny. She was buying an awful lot of canned cat food. I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo, but she completely ruined my good living room drapes... \n\nBut yes, the deep sadness and the voices and the messages, they were all happening at the exact same time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type). You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: I came in because even on my better days I can't shake the feeling that some people at church and in the walking club are watching me and putting little messages about me in the bulletin or in what they say.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Sometimes when the house is quiet I hear my name or a few words in my friend's voice from the kitchen or porch, clear enough that I answer before I remember nobody is there.\n- key: S021\n  name: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\n  description: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.\n  manifestation: When I try to explain things, I start with one thought about the mail or church and end up talking about quilt patterns, birds, and warnings, and then I can see people don't know what I'm getting at.\n- key: S022\n manifestation: There are mornings when I find myself standing by the front door with my purse in my hand for a long time, not going out and not really knowing what I was about to do.\n- key: S023\n hearing Shirley’s voice, have you found it harder to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been harder. I still live at home, you know, in the same house we bought in '85. It has that lovely wrap-around porch. But I just haven't been keeping up with my usual things. I used to go to my quilting gatherings every Wednesday. We'd sit around the big frame at the community center, all of us working on a different section. Helen always brought her famous lemon bars. She uses extra zest in the icing, that's her secret. I tried making them once, but I left them in the oven too long because the timer on my stove is broken. It just buzzes whenever it feels like it, completely unreliable. The track is made of that bouncy rubber material, so it's easy on the knees. I bought a special pair of New Balance shoes just for it, the white ones with the blue N on the side. They have excellent arch support. But they're just gathering dust in the closet now. I haven't even been doing much of my gardening, which is a shame because the weeds are just taking over the hostas. My daughter, Sarah, she noticed. But she insists on checking the fridge to see if I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? No, not controlling them... I saw Brenda there last week in the produce section. She was thumping a cantaloupe, trying to see if it was ripe. You really have to smell the stem end to know for sure, thumping doesn't do much for melons. It works for watermelons, though. My father taught me that. He used to grow the biggest watermelons in our backyard, the Jubilee kind with the dark green stripes. We'd eat them out on the patio and spit the seeds into the grass. The grass was always so green back then... But anyway, Brenda gave me this look, and I just know they're talking about me. And the church bulletin! I've been finding these little messages in it. Just little phrases that seem directed right at me, or things people say during fellowship hour that have a hidden meaning. It's very unsettling. We always have coffee and those little powdered sugar donuts after the service. The donuts always leave white dust all over my good black wool coat. I have a lint roller, the kind with the sticky paper you peel off, but it never seems to get it all. I buy the replacement rolls in bulk at the warehouse club. Do you have a membership there? When I was at my absolute lowest, just feeling so heavy and sad every single day... barely eating a thing and thinking I had just failed everyone in my life... that was exactly when I was hearing Shirley's voice the most, and believing all those church people were sending messages about me. It all happened right together. \n\nThe not eating was very unusual for me, because normally I love a good bowl of oatmeal in the morning. I buy the steel-cut oats, not those quick rolled ones. They take much longer to cook, you have to simmer them on the stove for a good twenty minutes, but the chewy texture is so much better. I like to put a little brown sugar and a splash of half-and-half in mine. The half-and-half comes in those little cardboard cartons, but they always seem to leak around the spout, don't they? His name tag says 'Kevin', but I'm pretty sure his real name is Kenneth because his mother came in once and called him Kenny. She was buying an awful lot of canned cat food. I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo, but she completely ruined my good living room drapes... \n\nBut yes, the deep sadness and the voices and the messages, they were all happening at the exact same time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave there been times when you were hearing the voice or noticing those messages even though you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, yes, actually. I mean, the sadness isn't always there every single minute. There was a spell... oh, maybe for several weeks... where I was actually getting up and getting dressed in my proper clothes, not just my housecoat. I have this lovely blue floral housecoat I bought at Mervyn's years ago, before they closed all the stores. It has pearl snap buttons down the front, which are so much easier for my hands than regular buttons. My arthritis flares up when it rains, you see. But anyway, I was getting dressed, eating a bit better—I even made myself a nice tuna salad sandwich on toasted rye bread. I always use a little sweet relish in my tuna, just a spoonful. Some people use dill relish, but I think sweet relish gives it a much better flavor. 

But even during that time, when I wasn't feeling so weighed down by the sadness, I was still hearing my name being called in the house when no one was there. Just out of nowhere. And I still felt certain that the people at church were hinting about me in the bulletin and during fellowship. Even when I felt perfectly fine in my mood, that part didn't go away. It makes me think I should maybe switch to the Methodist church on Elm Street. They have a beautiful stained glass window of a dove, and I hear their choir director is very talented. He used to teach music at the junior high...

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, even on my better days lately, I just can't shake this feeling I've been having. It's the people at my church, and also the ladies in my walking club. I just know they're watching me. They keep putting these little messages about me in the Sunday bulletin, or they slip things into what they say when we're out walking. \n\nWe usually walk around the reservoir, you know, the one over by the old highway? But last week it rained so much we had to walk at the indoor mall instead. I really don't care for the mall, the floors are too shiny and I always worry I'm going to slip in my sneakers. Plus, the smell from that soft pretzel stand is just overwhelming. My husband used to love those pretzels, though. He'd buy one every single time we went to Sears. It's such a shame Sears closed down, I used to get all my appliances there, and you just can't find that kind of customer service anymore. \n\nBut... oh, anyway. The messages. They just keep slipping these little hints about me into their conversations at the club and at church, and I just don't know why they're doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, sometimes I do. It usually happens when the house is just... completely quiet. I'll be sitting there, maybe doing a crossword puzzle, and I'll hear my name. Or just a few words, clear as day, coming from the kitchen or out on the back porch. And it sounds exactly like my friend Shirley's voice. It's so clear that I'll actually answer back, like, \"I'm in the living room, Shirley!\" before I realize... oh, she's not here. Nobody's here. \n\nShirley and I used to spend a lot of time on that porch. We'd sit out there with our iced tea—sweet tea, of course, the way she makes it with a pinch of baking soda so it doesn't get bitter. She swears by that trick, learned it from her grandmother who lived down in Georgia. Her grandmother had this beautiful old farmhouse with a wrap-around porch, the kind you just don't see being built anymore. They used real wood back then, not this composite stuff that looks like plastic. My son-in-law put a composite deck on his house, and I just think it gets far too hot on the feet in the summer... \n\nBut yes, I hear her voice, or sometimes just my name, when it's quiet.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these messages and hearing Shirley’s voice, have you found it harder to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been harder. I still live at home, you know, in the same house we bought in '85. It has that lovely wrap-around porch. But I just haven't been keeping up with my usual things. I used to go to my quilting gatherings every Wednesday. We'd sit around the big frame at the community center, all of us working on a different section. Helen always brought her famous lemon bars. She uses extra zest in the icing, that's her secret. I tried making them once, but I left them in the oven too long because the timer on my stove is broken. It just buzzes whenever it feels like it, completely unreliable. I really need to call the repairman, Mr. Henderson, but his truck is always parked outside the diner so I think he's mostly retired now. Anyway, I stopped going to quilting, and I haven't been to the walking club either. We used to walk the track at the high school, three miles every morning. The track is made of that bouncy rubber material, so it's easy on the knees. I bought a special pair of New Balance shoes just for it, the white ones with the blue N on the side. They have excellent arch support. But they're just gathering dust in the closet now. I haven't even been doing much of my gardening, which is a shame because the weeds are just taking over the hostas. My daughter, Sarah, she noticed. She's started coming over a lot more to check on my meals and make sure I'm getting to my appointments. She works at the bank downtown, the one with the big clock out front that chimes every hour. It's a very stressful job, so I feel bad that she has to worry about me on top of it all. But she insists on checking the fridge to see if I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? No, not controlling them... I don't feel like a puppet or anything. But I do feel... well, I came in today because even on my better days, when the sadness isn't so heavy, I just can't shake this awful feeling that people are watching me. Especially the people at church, and the ones from the walking club. I know I haven't been to the walking club in a while, but I still see them around town, you know? Like at the grocery store. I saw Brenda there last week in the produce section. She was thumping a cantaloupe, trying to see if it was ripe. You really have to smell the stem end to know for sure, thumping doesn't do much for melons. It works for watermelons, though. My father taught me that. He used to grow the biggest watermelons in our backyard, the Jubilee kind with the dark green stripes. We'd eat them out on the patio and spit the seeds into the grass. The grass was always so green back then... But anyway, Brenda gave me this look, and I just know they're talking about me. And the church bulletin! I've been finding these little messages in it. Just little phrases that seem directed right at me, or things people say during fellowship hour that have a hidden meaning. It's very unsettling. We always have coffee and those little powdered sugar donuts after the service. The donuts always leave white dust all over my good black wool coat. I have a lint roller, the kind with the sticky paper you peel off, but it never seems to get it all. I buy the replacement rolls in bulk at the warehouse club. Do you have a membership there? The savings on paper towels alone is worth the annual fee.\n\nDoctor: While these voices and messages have been happening, have you had a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. When I was at my absolute lowest, just feeling so heavy and sad every single day... barely eating a thing and thinking I had just failed everyone in my life... that was exactly when I was hearing Shirley's voice the most, and believing all those church people were sending messages about me. It all happened right together. \n\nThe not eating was very unusual for me, because normally I love a good bowl of oatmeal in the morning. I buy the steel-cut oats, not those quick rolled ones. They take much longer to cook, you have to simmer them on the stove for a good twenty minutes, but the chewy texture is so much better. I like to put a little brown sugar and a splash of half-and-half in mine. The half-and-half comes in those little cardboard cartons, but they always seem to leak around the spout, don't they? It leaves a sticky ring on the refrigerator shelf. I actually mentioned it to the manager at the grocery store, a nice young man who always wears a red tie. His name tag says 'Kevin', but I'm pretty sure his real name is Kenneth because his mother came in once and called him Kenny. She was buying an awful lot of canned cat food. I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo, but she completely ruined my good living room drapes... \n\nBut yes, the deep sadness and the voices and the messages, they were all happening at the exact same time.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the voice or noticing those messages even though you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I mean, the sadness isn't always there every single minute. There was a spell... oh, maybe for several weeks... where I was actually getting up and getting dressed in my proper clothes, not just my housecoat. I have this lovely blue floral housecoat I bought at Mervyn's years ago, before they closed all the stores. It has pearl snap buttons down the front, which are so much easier for my hands than regular buttons. My arthritis flares up when it rains, you see. But anyway, I was getting dressed, eating a bit better—I even made myself a nice tuna salad sandwich on toasted rye bread. I always use a little sweet relish in my tuna, just a spoonful. Some people use dill relish, but I think sweet relish gives it a much better flavor. \n\nBut even during that time, when I wasn't feeling so weighed down by the sadness, I was still hearing my name being called in the house when no one was there. Just out of nowhere. And I still felt certain that the people at church were hinting about me in the bulletin and during fellowship. Even when I felt perfectly fine in my mood, that part didn't go away. It makes me think I should maybe switch to the Methodist church on Elm Street. They have a beautiful stained glass window of a dove, and I hear their choir director is very talented. He used to teach music at the junior high...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I saw Brenda there last week in the produce section. She was thumping a cantaloupe, trying to see if it was ripe. I like to put a little brown sugar and a splash of half-and-half in mine. The half-and-half comes in those little cardboard cartons, but they always seem to leak around the spout, don't they? It leaves a sticky ring on the refrigerator shelf. I actually mentioned it to the manager at the grocery store, a nice young man who always wears a red tie. Yes, absolutely. Like I was just saying, there was a spell, oh, for several weeks at least. I wasn't feeling depressed at all. I was getting up, getting dressed, not feeling so weighed down by the sadness. I was even eating a bit better. But Shirley's voice... I wonder what happened to that watch... maybe her son took it. He lives in Arizona now, works in real estate. But yes, for a good few weeks there, my mood was just fine, but the voices and the messages from the church people didn't stop at all.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, even on my better days lately, I just can't shake this feeling I've been having. It's the people at my church, and also the ladies in my walking club. I just know they're watching me. They keep putting these little messages about me in the Sunday bulletin, or they slip things into what they say when we're out walking. \n\nWe usually walk around the reservoir, you know, the one over by the old highway? But last week it rained so much we had to walk at the indoor mall instead. I really don't care for the mall, the floors are too shiny and I always worry I'm going to slip in my sneakers. Plus, the smell from that soft pretzel stand is just overwhelming. My husband used to love those pretzels, though. He'd buy one every single time we went to Sears. It's such a shame Sears closed down, I used to get all my appliances there, and you just can't find that kind of customer service anymore. \n\nBut... oh, anyway. The messages. They just keep slipping these little hints about me into their conversations at the club and at church, and I just don't know why they're doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, sometimes I do. It usually happens when the house is just... completely quiet. I'll be sitting there, maybe doing a crossword puzzle, and I'll hear my name. Or just a few words, clear as day, coming from the kitchen or out on the back porch. And it sounds exactly like my friend Shirley's voice. It's so clear that I'll actually answer back, like, \"I'm in the living room, Shirley!\" before I realize... oh, she's not here. Nobody's here. \n\nShirley and I used to spend a lot of time on that porch. We'd sit out there with our iced tea—sweet tea, of course, the way she makes it with a pinch of baking soda so it doesn't get bitter. She swears by that trick, learned it from her grandmother who lived down in Georgia. Her grandmother had this beautiful old farmhouse with a wrap-around porch, the kind you just don't see being built anymore. They used real wood back then, not this composite stuff that looks like plastic. My son-in-law put a composite deck on his house, and I just think it gets far too hot on the feet in the summer... \n\nBut yes, I hear her voice, or sometimes just my name, when it's quiet.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed? I don't know if I'd say depressed most of the day, exactly. I mean, I've just been feeling... off, you know? Not quite myself. Sometimes I just sit in my recliner and don't feel like doing much of anything. It's that blue recliner I got at La-Z-Boy about five years ago. Oh, they had such a wonderful sale going on that weekend, it was Presidents' Day, I think. My daughter helped me pick it out because she said the floral pattern on my old one was just too outdated. But I loved that floral pattern, it matched the curtains my mother made for me when I first moved into the house. She was such a beautiful seamstress, my mother. She could look at a dress in a magazine and just make a pattern out of newspaper and sew it up perfectly. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had these delicate little seed pearls all over the bodice... it must have taken her weeks to sew them all on by hand. People just don't have that kind of patience for handiwork anymore, do they? Everyone's always in such a rush... But anyway, no, I haven't really noticed feeling terribly sad all day long, just sort of... drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I never forget a birthday or an anniversary. I still send out cards for all my nieces and nephews, and let me tell you, there are a lot of them now. My sister Margaret had five children, and they all went on to have at least three of their own. It gets so expensive buying stamps these days. Have you seen how much a book of stamps costs now? It’s just ridiculous. I remember when you could mail a letter for just a few cents. And the designs on the stamps aren't nearly as pretty as they used to be. I used to save the really nice ones, you know, the commemorative ones with the flowers or the old presidents. I had a whole album of them at one point. I wonder where that album went... I think I might have put it in the attic when we had the roof redone back in 2008. The roofers made such a mess of the yard, trampled right over my hydrangeas. I was so upset, those hydrangeas took years to establish. You have to get the soil acidity just right if you want them to be blue instead of pink. I use coffee grounds, myself. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after a medical illness or problem with your brain?\n\nPatient: Medical illness? Brain problem? No, goodness no, nothing like that. I haven't been in the hospital for anything serious since I had my gallbladder out, and that was... oh, it must have been the late nineties. Dr. Henderson did the surgery, such a nice man, though he always smelled a little bit like peppermint and cigar smoke. It's a funny combination, isn't it? He used to keep those little red and white peppermints in his pocket and hand them out, even to the adults. My husband, God rest his soul, used to love those mints. He'd always grab a handful from the bowl at the bank, too. The tellers knew him so well, they'd just slide the bowl right over to him when he walked in. That was back when they actually knew your name at the bank, before everything went to those machines and the internet. I still go inside to deposit my checks, I just don't trust those little slots on the ATM. You never know where the envelope is actually going, do you? It just gets sucked in and you just have to hope it gets to the right place. But anyway, no, my brain is fine, no medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or revved up?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Revved up? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get revved up about much these days. Well, except maybe when the neighborhood association decided to change the trash pickup day from Tuesday to Thursday. That certainly got me going. They just slipped a little note in the mailbox, no town hall meeting, no discussion. And Thursday is just terrible because that's the day the street sweeper comes, so if you put your cans out too early, they get in the way, and if you put them out too late, you miss the truck. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he put his out early one week and the street sweeper knocked his recycling bin right over. Cans and papers everywhere! It took him an hour to clean it up, and he's got a bad hip, you know. He had it replaced twice. The first time, they used some sort of metal joint that got recalled. Can you imagine? A recall on a part inside your own body! It's not like a toaster you can just take back to Sears. Not that Sears is even around anymore. I used to buy all my appliances there. They had that wonderful Kenmore brand, lasted forever. My first washing machine was a Kenmore, and it ran for twenty-two years without a single repair. Now you buy a machine and it's got all these computer chips and screens, and it breaks down in three years. It's just planned obsolescence, that's what it is. But anyway, what was the question? Revved up? No, I don't get energetic like that, just my normal self, really.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been having these experiences?\n\nPatient: How long? Oh, let me think... I suppose this has been going on since not long after my close friend died. That was about a year and five months ago now. It still feels like just yesterday, really. We used to do everything together. Every Tuesday we'd go down to the garden center just to look at the new arrivals. She absolutely loved orchids. I could never keep them alive myself, they're so finicky. You have to water them with just one ice cube a week, did you know that? Or at least, that's what the little plastic tag says. I always felt like an ice cube would freeze the poor roots. I prefer African violets, myself. I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill right now. They get just the right amount of morning sun there. The afternoon sun is too harsh, it scorches the leaves right off. I had to put up those sheer curtains to filter the light. I got them on sale at JCPenney before the one at the mall closed down. It's such a shame how all the good stores are closing. Now the mall is just phone repair kiosks and pretzel stands. I do like a soft pretzel every now and then, especially with that yellow mustard, but you can't exactly buy a decent pair of slacks at a pretzel stand, can you? You have to order everything online now, and you never know if it's going to fit. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the timing. Yes, about a year and five months, right after she passed away.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences been going on continuously for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Oh, yes, definitely more than a month. The voices, you know, and those messages... and just having so much trouble keeping my thoughts straight. It's been happening for about eleven months now. It started right around the time I was trying to organize my recipe box. I have this beautiful wooden box my mother gave me, it's got little painted daisies on the lid. She bought it at a craft fair in Vermont back in 1978. We took a bus tour up there to see the fall foliage. It was just breathtaking, all those reds and golds. We stopped at this little diner that served the most wonderful maple syrup over buttermilk pancakes. Real maple syrup, not that high-fructose corn syrup nonsense they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I always buy the glass bottles, even though they're heavier to carry home from the Piggly Wiggly. The bag boy, Tommy, he always offers to carry my groceries out, but I tell him I need the exercise. He's a sweet boy, going to community college for accounting. His mother must be so proud. But yes, eleven months of this... it's been quite a long time to deal with all that noise and confusion.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Well, yes, people do get a funny look on their faces sometimes. Like when I try to explain things, I might start with a thought about the mail—because the mail carrier, Frank, he's always running late on Thursdays. He says it's because of the circulars, but I think he just likes to stop and chat with Mrs. Higgins down the street. She has that lovely rose bush, you know, the one with the yellow blooms that smell like lemon? I tried growing yellow roses once, but the Japanese beetles just skeletonized the leaves. Horrible little pests. I had to buy these special traps, the ones with the little bags that hang from a metal stand. You're supposed to place them far away from the plants you want to protect, which seems counterintuitive, doesn't it? But it works, they all fly over to the bag. Anyway, or I might start talking about church, and then somehow I end up talking about quilt patterns—I'm very partial to the Double Wedding Ring pattern, it's so classic—and then birds, like the cardinals at my feeder, and then the warnings I get... and then I can just see people don't know what I'm getting at. They just stare at me, blinking, like they've lost the thread entirely. It's very frustrating, because it all connects perfectly in my head.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these messages and hearing Shirley’s voice, have you found it harder to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been harder. I still live at home, you know, in the same house we bought in '85. It has that lovely wrap-around porch. But I just haven't been keeping up with my usual things. I used to go to my quilting gatherings every Wednesday. We'd sit around the big frame at the community center, all of us working on a different section. Helen always brought her famous lemon bars. She uses extra zest in the icing, that's her secret. I tried making them once, but I left them in the oven too long because the timer on my stove is broken. It just buzzes whenever it feels like it, completely unreliable. I really need to call the repairman, Mr. Henderson, but his truck is always parked outside the diner so I think he's mostly retired now. Anyway, I stopped going to quilting, and I haven't been to the walking club either. We used to walk the track at the high school, three miles every morning. The track is made of that bouncy rubber material, so it's easy on the knees. I bought a special pair of New Balance shoes just for it, the white ones with the blue N on the side. They have excellent arch support. But they're just gathering dust in the closet now. I haven't even been doing much of my gardening, which is a shame because the weeds are just taking over the hostas. My daughter, Sarah, she noticed. She's started coming over a lot more to check on my meals and make sure I'm getting to my appointments. She works at the bank downtown, the one with the big clock out front that chimes every hour. It's a very stressful job, so I feel bad that she has to worry about me on top of it all. But she insists on checking the fridge to see if I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? No, not controlling them... I don't feel like a puppet or anything. But I do feel... well, I came in today because even on my better days, when the sadness isn't so heavy, I just can't shake this awful feeling that people are watching me. Especially the people at church, and the ones from the walking club. I know I haven't been to the walking club in a while, but I still see them around town, you know? Like at the grocery store. I saw Brenda there last week in the produce section. She was thumping a cantaloupe, trying to see if it was ripe. You really have to smell the stem end to know for sure, thumping doesn't do much for melons. It works for watermelons, though. My father taught me that. He used to grow the biggest watermelons in our backyard, the Jubilee kind with the dark green stripes. We'd eat them out on the patio and spit the seeds into the grass. The grass was always so green back then... But anyway, Brenda gave me this look, and I just know they're talking about me. And the church bulletin! I've been finding these little messages in it. Just little phrases that seem directed right at me, or things people say during fellowship hour that have a hidden meaning. It's very unsettling. We always have coffee and those little powdered sugar donuts after the service. The donuts always leave white dust all over my good black wool coat. I have a lint roller, the kind with the sticky paper you peel off, but it never seems to get it all. I buy the replacement rolls in bulk at the warehouse club. Do you have a membership there? The savings on paper towels alone is worth the annual fee.\n\nDoctor: While these voices and messages have been happening, have you had a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. When I was at my absolute lowest, just feeling so heavy and sad every single day... barely eating a thing and thinking I had just failed everyone in my life... that was exactly when I was hearing Shirley's voice the most, and believing all those church people were sending messages about me. It all happened right together. \n\nThe not eating was very unusual for me, because normally I love a good bowl of oatmeal in the morning. I buy the steel-cut oats, not those quick rolled ones. They take much longer to cook, you have to simmer them on the stove for a good twenty minutes, but the chewy texture is so much better. I like to put a little brown sugar and a splash of half-and-half in mine. The half-and-half comes in those little cardboard cartons, but they always seem to leak around the spout, don't they? It leaves a sticky ring on the refrigerator shelf. I actually mentioned it to the manager at the grocery store, a nice young man who always wears a red tie. His name tag says 'Kevin', but I'm pretty sure his real name is Kenneth because his mother came in once and called him Kenny. She was buying an awful lot of canned cat food. I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo, but she completely ruined my good living room drapes... \n\nBut yes, the deep sadness and the voices and the messages, they were all happening at the exact same time.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the voice or noticing those messages even though you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I mean, the sadness isn't always there every single minute. There was a spell... oh, maybe for several weeks... where I was actually getting up and getting dressed in my proper clothes, not just my housecoat. I have this lovely blue floral housecoat I bought at Mervyn's years ago, before they closed all the stores. It has pearl snap buttons down the front, which are so much easier for my hands than regular buttons. My arthritis flares up when it rains, you see. But anyway, I was getting dressed, eating a bit better—I even made myself a nice tuna salad sandwich on toasted rye bread. I always use a little sweet relish in my tuna, just a spoonful. Some people use dill relish, but I think sweet relish gives it a much better flavor. \n\nBut even during that time, when I wasn't feeling so weighed down by the sadness, I was still hearing my name being called in the house when no one was there. Just out of nowhere. And I still felt certain that the people at church were hinting about me in the bulletin and during fellowship. Even when I felt perfectly fine in my mood, that part didn't go away. It makes me think I should maybe switch to the Methodist church on Elm Street. They have a beautiful stained glass window of a dove, and I hear their choir director is very talented. He used to teach music at the junior high...\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a stretch of at least two weeks when you were hearing Shirley’s voice or believing people were sending you messages, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Like I was just saying, there was a spell, oh, for several weeks at least. I wasn't feeling depressed at all. I was getting up, getting dressed, not feeling so weighed down by the sadness. I was even eating a bit better. But Shirley's voice... I was still hearing my name called out in the house, clear as a bell, even though I was completely alone. And I still felt absolutely certain that the people at church were hinting about me in the bulletin. \n\nIt’s just so strange, isn't it? Hearing things when you're alone. The house is usually so quiet. Well, except for the refrigerator. It has this hum, a low sort of vibration. It’s an older model, a Frigidaire, almond-colored. You don't see appliances in almond much anymore, do you? Everything is stainless steel now. I don't care for stainless steel, it shows every single fingerprint. My neighbor, Helen, she remodeled her whole kitchen with stainless steel. She spent a fortune. Her husband left her a very comfortable pension when he passed. He worked for the railroad for forty years. They gave him a gold watch when he retired. I wonder what happened to that watch... maybe her son took it. He lives in Arizona now, works in real estate. But yes, for a good few weeks there, my mood was just fine, but the voices and the messages from the church people didn't stop at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
